    Sarah Johnson: "Our hospital values safety, respect, accountability, and teamwork. Which of those values connects most strongly with your nursing practice?"
    Ezra: "Safety connects most strongly with my practice because every nursing action should protect the patient. For example, I always verify patient identity, medication orders, allergies, and changes in condition before acting. I also believe safety depends on teamwork because speaking up, asking questions, and communicating clearly can prevent errors."
    Michael Patel: "Can you give us one strength that would help you succeed on this unit and one area you are actively improving?"
    Ezra: "One strength is that I stay calm and organized when the unit is busy. I make priorities, reassess patients, and communicate early if I need support. One area I am improving is speaking more naturally in English during high-pressure situations. I practice clinical conversations, handoff reports, and interview responses so that I can communicate clearly and confidently with patients and the healthcare team."
  SCENE 2: Scene 2: Behavioral Questions, Teamwork, and Communication
    Sarah Johnson: "Ezra, I would like to ask about teamwork now. Tell me about a time when you worked with a difficult team member or had a communication challenge."
    Ezra: "In one situation, a coworker seemed frustrated during a busy shift and gave very short responses when I asked about a patient. I noticed that the communication was becoming unclear, so I stayed calm and asked to clarify the key information using specific questions. Later, when the unit became less busy, I spoke with the coworker privately and respectfully. I said that I wanted us to communicate clearly because patient safety depended on it. After that conversation, we worked more smoothly, and I learned that direct but respectful communication can prevent misunderstanding."
    Michael Patel: "What did you do to make sure the patient was not affected by that communication issue?"
    Ezra: "I verified the patient's current orders, assessment findings, and pending tasks in the chart instead of relying only on verbal information. I also updated the charge nurse when I needed clarification. Most importantly, I repeated back important details to confirm that I understood correctly. That helped keep the patient's care consistent and safe."
    Sarah Johnson: "Tell us about a time when you received feedback from a supervisor. How did you respond?"
    Ezra: "A supervisor once told me that my clinical documentation was accurate but could be more concise. At first, I felt a little disappointed because I was trying to be thorough. However, I understood that clear documentation should include relevant information without unnecessary details. I reviewed examples, asked for guidance, and adjusted my notes to be more focused. After a few weeks, my supervisor told me my documentation had improved."
    Michael Patel: "That is a good example. How do you usually handle stress during a busy shift?"
    Ezra: "I manage stress by focusing on priorities instead of trying to do everything at once. I quickly identify unstable patients, time-sensitive medications, safety risks, and tasks that can wait. I also communicate with the charge nurse when I need help. Emotionally, I take a short breath, keep my voice calm, and remind myself that my job is to take the next safe step."
    Sarah Johnson: "Imagine a family member is angry because they feel no one is updating them. What would you say and do?"
    Ezra: "I would first acknowledge their concern and speak in a calm tone. I might say, I can see that you are worried, and I am sorry you feel left out of the updates. I would check what information they need, confirm what I am allowed to share based on privacy rules, and then provide a clear update within my nursing scope. If they need information from the provider, I would contact the provider and let the family know when they can expect follow-up."
    Michael Patel: "What if the family continues to raise their voice even after you explain the plan?"
    Ezra: "I would continue to stay calm and set respectful boundaries. I would say that I want to help, but we need to speak respectfully so that I can understand their concerns. If the situation feels unsafe or continues to escalate, I would involve the charge nurse or security according to hospital policy. My goal would be to protect the patient, the family, and the staff while still responding with empathy."
  SCENE 3: Scene 3: Clinical Judgment, Patient Safety, and Closing
    Michael Patel: "Ezra, let us move into a clinical scenario. You are caring for four patients, and one patient suddenly becomes short of breath with oxygen saturation dropping to 86 percent. What would you do first?"
    Ezra: "I would assess the patient immediately because a change in breathing is urgent. I would check airway, breathing, circulation, level of consciousness, vital signs, lung sounds, and the patient's position. I would raise the head of the bed, apply oxygen according to protocol, and call for help if the patient is unstable. I would notify the provider or rapid response team depending on the severity, and I would continue monitoring and documenting the patient's response."
    Sarah Johnson: "What information would you include when you call the provider or rapid response team?"
    Ezra: "I would use SBAR. For the situation, I would state that the patient has acute shortness of breath and oxygen saturation is 86 percent. For background, I would include the diagnosis, recent surgery or infection if relevant, oxygen baseline, and important medical history. For assessment, I would report vital signs, lung sounds, mental status, pain, skin color, and interventions already done. For recommendation, I would ask for immediate evaluation and any needed orders."
    Michael Patel: "Now imagine you are preparing to give a medication, and you notice the dose seems much higher than usual. The provider is busy and says to give it anyway. How would you handle that?"
    Ezra: "I would not give a medication if I believe it may be unsafe. I would respectfully explain my concern and clarify the order using objective information, such as the usual dose range, the patient's renal function, allergies, vital signs, or lab results. If the concern is not resolved, I would follow the chain of command and contact the charge nurse, pharmacist, or another provider according to policy. Patient safety is the priority, and I would document the communication appropriately."
    Sarah Johnson: "Some candidates say they would just trust the provider. Why is your approach important?"
    Ezra: "Nursing responsibility includes independent safety checks. Providers write orders, but nurses are accountable for assessing whether the medication is safe for the patient at that moment. Respectful questioning is not disrespectful; it is part of teamwork and error prevention. If the dose is correct, clarification protects everyone. If the dose is wrong, speaking up may prevent serious harm."
    Michael Patel: "How do you prevent infection during routine patient care when the unit is very busy?"
    Ezra: "I prevent infection by keeping basic practices consistent even when I am busy. I perform hand hygiene before and after patient contact, use the correct personal protective equipment, clean equipment between patients, follow isolation precautions, and maintain aseptic technique for lines, wounds, and catheters. I also educate patients and families because infection prevention is a team effort."
    Sarah Johnson: "We are almost finished. Ezra, do you have any questions for us about the unit or the hospital?"
    Ezra: "Yes, thank you. I would like to ask about the orientation process for nurses who are new to your hospital. I am also interested in how your unit supports professional development, such as mentorship, continuing education, or specialty certification."
    Sarah Johnson: "Those are excellent questions. We provide a structured orientation with a preceptor, skills validation, and regular check-ins during the first several months."
    Michael Patel: "We also encourage nurses to attend education sessions and work toward certification after they gain experience on the unit."
    Ezra: "That sounds very supportive. I appreciate hearing that your unit values learning and safe transition into practice. Thank you again for your time today. I enjoyed speaking with both of you and learning more about the position."
    Sarah Johnson: "Thank you, Ezra. We appreciate your thoughtful answers and your focus on patient safety and teamwork."

  KEY PHRASES
  Practice responding in these clinical situations using the correct key phrases.
  1. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse says, “Can you summarise the main safety concern for this patient?”  → correct: "The main safety concern is falls risk due to dizziness on standing, so please supervise mobilisation and keep the call bell within reach."
  2. [Patient] A patient says, “I feel worse after taking that tablet. My lips feel swollen.”  → correct: "Stop taking any further doses for now; I will assess you immediately and escalate this as a possible allergic reaction."
  3. [Doctor] The doctor asks, “What is your recommendation?”  → correct: "I recommend urgent review now because the blood pressure is falling and the patient is becoming more confused."
  4. [Patient] A patient says, “I don’t understand why I need this injection.”  → correct: "This injection helps reduce the risk of blood clots while you are less mobile; I can explain the benefits and side effects before you decide."
  5. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “Has the wound dressing been checked?”  → correct: "Yes, it was checked at fourteen hundred; it is clean and dry with no new bleeding noted."
  6. [Patient] A patient says, “Can I get out of bed by myself?”  → correct: "Please call before getting up; I need to check your balance first because you felt light-headed earlier."
  7. [Doctor] The doctor says, “Why are you concerned about infection?”  → correct: "The patient has a rising temperature, increased heart rate, new confusion, and reduced urine output."
  8. [Patient] A patient says, “I feel short of breath.”  → correct: "I will sit you upright, check your oxygen level and breathing rate, and call for urgent support if needed."
  9. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “What still needs to be completed before discharge?”  → correct: "The patient needs medication counselling, removal of the cannula, and written instructions reviewed with family present."
  10. [Patient] A patient says, “I want to refuse this procedure.”  → correct: "You have the right to refuse; I will check your understanding, answer questions, and inform the doctor promptly."
  11. [Doctor] The doctor asks, “Has the medication allergy been confirmed?”  → correct: "Yes, the patient reports facial swelling after penicillin, and it is documented on the medication chart and wristband."
  12. [Patient] A patient says, “My pain is eight out of ten.”  → correct: "I will assess the pain location and character, check what analgesia is due, and reassess after treatment."
  13. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse says, “What should I monitor most closely overnight?”  → correct: "Monitor respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, sedation score, and pain score after the opioid doses."
  14. [Doctor] The doctor asks, “Can you read back the verbal order?”  → correct: "To confirm, give ceftriaxone one gram intravenously once daily, starting now, after checking allergies."
  15. [Patient] A patient says, “I feel embarrassed about needing help to wash.”  → correct: "I understand this may feel uncomfortable; I will protect your privacy and support you only as much as needed."
  16. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “Is the patient safe to eat and drink?”  → correct: "Not yet; the swallow screen is pending, so keep the patient nil by mouth until it is completed."

    Ezra: "I was interested because your hospital has a strong reputation for patient-centered care, evidence-based practice, and professional development for nurses. I also appreciate that your team values communication and safety, because those are very important to me as a nurse."
    Emily Rodriguez: "You mentioned patient-centered care. Can you give an example of how you show that in your daily nursing practice?"
    Ezra: "Yes. I try to understand not only the patient's diagnosis but also their concerns, culture, family situation, and learning needs. For example, when I teach a patient about discharge medications, I ask what they already know, check their understanding, and adjust my explanation so it is clear and practical for them."
    Sarah Johnson: "That is helpful. What would you say is one of your biggest strengths as a nurse?"
    Ezra: "One of my strengths is staying calm and organized when the unit is busy. I prioritize patient safety first, reassess my patients frequently, and communicate early if I need support. I also try to help my coworkers when I am able, because teamwork affects patient outcomes."
    Emily Rodriguez: "Thank you, Ezra. We also like to ask about growth areas. What is one weakness you are working on?"
    Ezra: "One area I am improving is speaking more confidently in large team discussions. I used to hesitate if many people were present, but I have been practicing concise communication using SBAR and speaking up earlier when I notice a safety concern."
  SCENE 2: Scene 2: Clinical Judgment, Patient Safety, and Prioritization
    Michael Patel: "Hello, Ezra. I am Michael Patel, the clinical educator for the unit. I would like to ask a few clinical and situational questions."
    Ezra: "Hello, Michael. That sounds good. I am ready."
    Michael Patel: "You start your shift and receive four patients. One has new shortness of breath, one is asking for pain medication, one needs discharge teaching, and one has a scheduled antibiotic due soon. How would you prioritize?"
    Ezra: "I would assess the patient with new shortness of breath first because that could indicate an immediate airway or breathing problem. I would check vital signs, oxygen saturation, lung sounds, level of distress, and notify the provider or rapid response team if needed. After stabilizing that patient, I would address the antibiotic because timing is important, manage the pain medication safely, and complete discharge teaching when the patient is stable and ready to learn."
    Michael Patel: "Good. If the patient with shortness of breath had an oxygen saturation of 84 percent, what would you do right away?"
    Ezra: "I would stay with the patient, raise the head of the bed, apply oxygen according to the facility protocol, and reassess the oxygen saturation and respiratory effort. I would call for help, notify the provider, and prepare to activate rapid response if the patient did not improve quickly or showed signs of deterioration."
    Sarah Johnson: "Let us talk about medication safety. Tell me how you prevent medication errors during a busy shift."
    Ezra: "I follow the rights of medication administration, verify patient identity with two identifiers, check allergies, review the order carefully, and clarify anything that does not look safe. I also avoid rushing during medication preparation, use barcode scanning when available, and educate the patient so they can help confirm what they are receiving."
    Sarah Johnson: "Suppose a physician gives you a verbal order that seems like an unusually high dose. What would you say?"
    Ezra: "I would remain respectful but speak up. I might say, 'Doctor, I want to clarify the dose because it seems higher than expected for this patient. Could we review the order together?' If I still had concerns, I would follow the chain of command and involve the pharmacist or charge nurse to protect the patient."
    Michael Patel: "That is a patient safety mindset. Now imagine a family member is upset and says no one is listening to them. How would you handle that conversation?"
    Ezra: "I would listen without interrupting, acknowledge their frustration, and speak calmly. I would say something like, 'I can see this has been very stressful for you, and I want to understand your concerns.' Then I would clarify the issue, provide information within my scope, and involve the provider or charge nurse if they needed more detailed answers."
    Michael Patel: "How do you balance empathy with keeping boundaries when a family is demanding a lot of your time?"
    Ezra: "I try to be respectful and clear. I would acknowledge the importance of their concerns, explain that I need to continue caring for all my patients, and set a realistic time to return with updates. If appropriate, I would also suggest one family spokesperson to improve communication."
  SCENE 3: Scene 3: Teamwork, Ethics, Leadership, and Closing Questions
    Emily Rodriguez: "Ezra, I would like to ask about teamwork. Tell us about a time when you had a conflict or disagreement with a coworker."
    Ezra: "In one situation, a coworker and I had different opinions about how to manage a patient who was becoming more confused. I felt the patient needed closer monitoring, while my coworker thought the patient was just tired. I calmly explained my assessment findings, listened to my coworker's perspective, and suggested that we reassess the patient together. After reassessment, we agreed to notify the provider, and the patient received further evaluation."
    Sarah Johnson: "What did you learn from that experience?"
    Ezra: "I learned that disagreement can be productive if we focus on the patient instead of personal opinions. Clear communication, objective assessment data, and respectful teamwork helped us make a safer decision."
    Michael Patel: "Let us move to an ethical situation. If a patient refuses a treatment that the medical team strongly recommends, what would you do?"
    Ezra: "I would first assess whether the patient understands the treatment, the benefits, and the risks of refusing. I would respect the patient's autonomy and avoid pressuring them. I would notify the provider, document the conversation according to policy, and continue to provide supportive care."
    Michael Patel: "What if the family asks you to convince the patient to accept the treatment?"
    Ezra: "I would explain that my role is to provide accurate information and support the patient's informed decision. I could help facilitate a conversation between the patient, family, and provider, but I would not manipulate or pressure the patient."
    Sarah Johnson: "This position can be stressful. How do you manage stress during a difficult shift?"
    Ezra: "During a difficult shift, I focus on priorities, communicate early, and ask for help when needed. I use brief mental resets, such as taking a deep breath before entering the next room, so I can stay present with each patient. After the shift, I reflect on what went well and what I can improve."
    Emily Rodriguez: "Where do you see yourself professionally in the next three to five years?"
    Ezra: "In the next three to five years, I want to become a highly reliable staff nurse on the unit, continue improving my clinical judgment, and possibly take on preceptor or charge nurse responsibilities when I am ready. I also want to continue developing my English communication so I can advocate confidently for patients and collaborate well with the team."
    Sarah Johnson: "Thank you, Ezra. Before we finish, what questions do you have for us?"
    Ezra: "Thank you. I would like to ask about the orientation process for new nurses on this unit. How do you support nurses as they transition into the team and learn the hospital's policies and workflow?"
    Sarah Johnson: "That is a thoughtful question. Our orientation includes a dedicated preceptor, weekly check-ins, skills validation, and gradual patient assignments based on progress."
    Ezra: "That sounds very supportive. I also wanted to ask how your team promotes patient safety and open communication when nurses have concerns."
    Michael Patel: "We encourage nurses to speak up, use SBAR, report near misses, and participate in safety huddles. We want staff to feel comfortable raising concerns early."
    Ezra: "I appreciate hearing that. Thank you all for your time today. I am very interested in the opportunity to contribute to your team and provide safe, compassionate care to your patients."
    Emily Rodriguez: "Thank you, Ezra. We enjoyed speaking with you, and we will follow up with you soon about the next steps."

  KEY PHRASES
  Practice responding in these clinical situations using the correct key phrases.
  1. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “What is the main concern for this patient during the next shift?”  → correct: "The main concern is the change in respiratory status, so please prioritise repeat observations and monitor for deterioration."
  2. [Patient] A patient says, “I do not understand why you keep checking my blood pressure.”  → correct: "We check it because changes can show how your body is responding, and it helps us keep you safe."
  3. [Doctor] The doctor asks, “Why are you escalating this patient now?”  → correct: "I am escalating because the patient has a new clinical change and needs timely medical review."
  4. [Patient] A patient says, “I feel dizzy when I stand up.”  → correct: "Please sit back down while I check your observations and assess whether it is safe for you to mobilise."
  5. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “Has the family been updated?”  → correct: "Yes, the patient gave permission, and the family was updated about the current plan of care."
  6. [Doctor] A doctor asks, “What do you recommend?” during an escalation call.  → correct: "I recommend an urgent review because the patient’s condition has changed from baseline."
  7. [Patient] A patient says, “Can I take my own tablets from home?”  → correct: "Please do not take them yet; I need to check them against your prescription and confirm they are safe to use."
  8. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “What should I watch for after the procedure?”  → correct: "Please monitor for increased pain, bleeding, swelling, fever, or any change in observations."
  9. [Patient] A patient refuses a medication and says, “I do not want it.”  → correct: "That is your choice; can I explain what it is for and check your concerns before I document your decision?"
  10. [Doctor] The doctor asks, “What is the patient’s current level of consciousness?”  → correct: "The patient is alert, responds appropriately, and is oriented to person, place, and time."
  11. [Patient] A patient says, “My pain is getting worse.”  → correct: "I will assess your pain now, check when your last pain relief was given, and review what options are prescribed."
  12. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “Is this patient safe to mobilise alone?”  → correct: "No, the patient needs assistance because they felt dizzy on standing and remain at risk of falls."
  13. [Doctor] A doctor asks for a concise SBAR opening.  → correct: "Situation: I am calling about a new deterioration in breathing in a patient on the medical ward."
  14. [Patient] A patient asks, “Will you tell me before you do anything?”  → correct: "Yes, I will explain each step, check your consent, and give you time to ask questions."
  15. [Incoming Nurse] The incoming nurse asks, “What has already been done for the patient today?”  → correct: "The patient has had observations repeated, symptoms assessed, treatment reviewed, and the doctor informed of the change."
  16. [Patient] A patient says, “I am embarrassed to ask for help getting to the toilet.”  → correct: "Please do not worry; your safety is important, and I will help you maintain your privacy and dignity."

  4. "Abdomen" [/ŋ/] — Palpate the abdomen for tenderness.

  VOCABULARY
  Focus on using these vocabularies correctly. Pay attention to the meaning and appropriate usage.
  1. The nurse was responsible for the patient's _____.  → answer: "monitoring"
  2. We need to assess the _____ of the wound.  → answer: "dressing"
  3. The physician ordered a _____ of the patient's oxygen saturation.  → answer: "reading"

  ROLEPLAY
  Improve your speaking and conversational skills in these difficult scenarios. Make your mistakes here, not during your shift.
  SCENE 1: Handover Report
    Nurse Eklan: "Good morning. I'm handing over care for Mr. Henderson in room 302. He's a 72-year-old male admitted yesterday with pneumonia."
    Patient Mr. Henderson: "Good morning. How has he been doing overnight?"
    Nurse Eklan: "His breathing has been a concern. We've been providing supplemental oxygen at 4 liters per minute via nasal cannula, and his saturation has been ranging between 90% and 94%."
    Patient Mr. Henderson: "Understood. Any changes in his cough or sputum production?"
    Nurse Eklan: "His cough is still productive, with thick, yellow sputum. We've encouraged deep breathing and coughing exercises every two hours. He's also on IV antibiotics."
    Patient Mr. Henderson: "Thank you. I'll continue monitoring his respiratory status closely."

  KEY PHRASES
  Practice responding in these clinical situations using the correct key phrases.
  1. [Nurse] A patient asks, 'Nurse, I'm feeling very short of breath. Can you check on me?'  → correct: "I will assess your breathing and oxygen saturation immediately."
  2. [Outgoing Nurse] A colleague asks during handover, 'What's the patient's current oxygen level?'  → correct: "His oxygen saturation is currently reading 92% on room air."
  3. [Nurse] A doctor asks, 'Can you give me an update on the patient's wound healing?'  → correct: "The wound is clean, dry, and intact, with no signs of infection noted."
